Title: Absence of blow-up in the 3D Navier-Stokes equations with transport noise

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.15140v1

Published Time: Fri, 17 Jul 2026 01:23:19 GMT

Number of Pages: 125

Markdown Content:
ABSENCE OF BLOW-UP IN THE 3D NAVIER-STOKES EQUATIONS WITH TRANSPORT NOISE 

ANTONIO AGRESTI 

Abstract. Establishing the global-in-time smoothness of solutions to the 3D Navier-Stokes equations (NSEs) with large initial data remains a long-standing open problem. In this paper, we prove that the 3D NSEs driven by a suitably chosen transport noise—a physically motivated stochastic perturbation—admit global-in-time smooth solutions with high probability. This regularizing effect holds uniformly for initial data in arbitrarily large balls of subcritical function spaces with positive smoothness.

1. Introduction 

In this paper, we establish a regularizing effect of transport noise on the Navier-Stokes equations (NSEs) on the three-dimensional torus T3 “ R3{Z3:(1.1)

Finally, θ “ p θkqkPZ30 P ℓ2 are the noise coefficients, and μ ą 0 is the noise intensity. The physical motivation for such stochastic perturbations is discussed in Subsection 1.2 below. A long-standing conjecture in stochastic fluid mechanics suggests that physically motivated stochastic perturbations may improve the well-posedness theory for the 3D NSEs [20, 43, 48]. This potential regularizing effect might indicate, from a phys-ical point of view, that small-scale turbulence can stabilize the corresponding fluid dynamics (see Subsection 1.2). We establish the existence of global-in-time smooth solutions to the stochastic 3D NSEs, provided that the coefficients and the noise intensity are appropriately chosen. An informal version of our main result, Theorem 4.1, reads as follows. 

Theorem 1.1 (Global-in-time regularity of 3D NSEs with transport noise – Informal statement of Theorem 4.1) . For all γ ą 1{2, M ě 1 and ε P p 0, 1q, there exist μ ą 0

and θ P ℓ2pZ30q such that, for any divergence-free vector field u0 satisfying 

}u0}Hγ pT3;R3q ď M, 

there exists a unique smooth solution u to the 3D NSEs with transport noise (1.1) 

that is global in time with probability at least 1 ´ ε.

In the deterministic setting ( μ “ 0), proving global smoothness for arbitrarily large initial data is one of the Millennium Prize Problems [42]. Establishing Theorem 1.1 is a highly nontrivial task, and it yields the first regu-larization by noise result for a fully physical stochastic perturbation of the 3D NSEs. A primary challenge in establishing this regularization phenomenon is that transport noise does not affect the energy balance. Indeed, smooth solutions to (1.1) satisfy the pathwise energy equality :(1.2) 12

In particular, one recovers the same energy balance that holds in the absence of noise ( μ “ 0) independently of the noise intensity μ ą 0, and coefficients θ “ p θkqkPZ30 .The key to overcoming the limitations of standard energy methods lies in capturing the mixing properties of transport noise. This mechanism provides a mathematical framework to extract a macroscopic regularizing effect—aligning with the classical Boussinesq hypothesis [22, 44]—without altering the fundamental energy balance (1.2). The resulting enhanced dissipation on the Navier-Stokes dynamics is quantified in Corollary 4.3. The proof of Theorem 1.1 is outlined in Figure 1, and the main ingredients are presented in Section 2. The underlying analysis extends to subcritical initial data with positive Besov regularity (see Theorem 4.1): (1.3) }u0}B1´2{pq,p pT3;R3q ď M where p, q P p 2, 8q satisfy 2

For instance, this accommodates initial data in W ε, 3pT3; R3q for any ε ą 0. In the remaining part of the introduction, we first compare our results with the deterministic case (Subsection 1.1), then we briefly discuss the physical relevance of transport noise in stochastic fluid mechanics (Subsection 1.2), and finally, we outline some previous works on regularization by noise (Subsection 1.3). 1.1. Comparison with the deterministic 3D NSEs. The literature on the de-terministic 3D NSEs is vast, and the reader is referred to [80] for a comprehensive overview. While the fundamental problem of global regularity remains open [42], sev-eral recent breakthroughs have revealed various pathological behaviors of the deter-ministic 3D NSEs, and, in particular, have shown that energy methods are insufficient to rule out singularities. In the context of strong solutions, Tao [102] constructed an averaged version of the 3D NSEs that exhibits the same energy cancellation as the true 3D NSEs, yet admits strong solutions that blow up in finite time. Moreover, in [102, Subsection 1.3], the author discusses how the mechanism driving this singularity formation might also be adapted to prove finite-time blow-up in the true deterministic NSEs. In the context of weak solutions, several non-uniqueness results have been discov-ered. Notably, the breakthrough development of convex integration techniques by De Lellis and Sz´ ekelyhidi [36] has led to major results. Indeed, these techniques laid the groundwork for Isett’s proof of Onsager’s conjecture for the Euler equations [71], and subsequently enabled Buckmaster and Vicol [26] to prove the flexible non-uniqueness of weak solutions with finite kinetic energy (i.e., u P L8 

> t

pL2

> x

q). More recently, non-uniqueness of Leray-Hopf weak solutions (i.e., u P L8 

> t

pL2

> x

q X L2 

> t

pH1 

> x

q) was established for the forced 3D NSEs by Albritton, Bru´ e and Colombo [11], in accordance with the predictions of Jia and ˇSver´ ak [73]. A possible extension to the unforced 3D NSEs has been recently published in the preprint [67]. Finally, non-uniqueness phenomena in the class of finite-kinetic-energy weak solu-tions have been shown to persist even in the presence of transport noise, as established ABSENCE OF BLOW-UP IN 3D NSEs WITH TRANSPORT NOISE 5

by Hofmanov´ a, Lange, and Pappalettera [62] in the Euler case, and by Pappalettera [96] for NSEs via convex integration techniques. While these results show that transport noise cannot restore the uniqueness of weak solutions with finite kinetic energy [20], Theorem 1.1 establishes that it prevents the finite-time blow-up of strong solutions with high probability. 1.2. Physical motivation. Transport-type stochastic perturbations of the NSEs can be physically derived through several approaches: via homogenization of two-scale systems [38], by a two-scale ansatz [49, Subsection 1.2] and [3, Subsection 1.2], through principles of location uncertainty [37, 91], and from Newton’s law [92, 93]. In the latter approach, (1.1) is derived from the momentum balance, assuming that a fluid particle ϕtpxq located at x at the initial time (i.e., ϕ0pxq “ x) evolves according to the following stochastic differential equation: ddt ϕtpxq “ upt, ϕ tpxqq `

The physical intuition behind this decomposition is the so-called separation of scales 

(see e.g., [25, 51, 92]). In a turbulent regime, the fluid is modeled as a two-scale system: a large regular component u, reflecting our (continuum) observation scale, and a small, highly time-oscillatory turbulent component ap3μq{ 2 ř 

> k,α

θkσk,α ˝ 9W k,α t ,reflecting the microscopic nature of the fluid. Although such a strict separation of scales remains a modeling idealization, this reasoning is widely used in the study of real fluids [43, 44, 90]. Transport noise is not the only physically relevant stochastic perturbation of fluid models; indeed, alternative models emerge depending on the specific physical quanti-ties being conserved (see, e.g., [37, 51, 63, 66] and references therein). Nevertheless, transport noise is distinguished by two structural features: it is both energy pre-serving (as seen in (1.2)) and scaling invariant (see Subsection 2.2). Moreover, we note that transport noise serves as a fundamental building block for many physically relevant stochastic perturbations of fluid models. 1.3. Related works. The question of whether noise can prevent the formation of singularities in the 3D NSEs, and more generally in fluid equations, has inspired an extensive body of research over the last few decades. While a comprehensive overview is beyond our scope, we highlight a few key results. For weak solutions to the 3D NSEs with additive noise, a stochastic Caffarelli-Kohn-Nirenberg theory was established by Flandoli and Romito [52], proving the almost sure absence of singularities at any fixed time. In the same setting, Markov selections with the strong Feller property were obtained by the same authors in [53], relying on the analysis of the infinite-dimensional Kolmogorov equation by Da Prato and Debussche [34] (see [65] for a result on non-uniqueness in law via convex integration). In the case of multiplicative noise depending linearly on the velocity, global well-posedness for the Euler equations was proven by Glatt-Holtz and Vicol [59]. More recently, and for the 2D Euler equations with rough Kraichnan noise, Coghi and Maurelli [29] established uniqueness beyond the Yudovich class (see also [19, 74]). 6 ANTONIO AGRESTI 

The regularizing potential of transport noise was first discovered in the seminal work of Flandoli, Gubinelli, and Priola [48] on linear transport equations. In this setting, the authors proved well-posedness for drifts with H¨ older regularity below the threshold of the classical DiPerna-Lions theory [40] (see [24] for the optimality of the latter). However, as noted in [48], transport noise with spatially independent coefficients cannot, in general, prevent blow-up in nonlinear fluid equations. A fundamental step in understanding the regularization properties of transport noise was made by Galeati in [55]. In this work, the author showed that solutions to transport equations with suitably chosen transport noise converge to solutions of a deterministic heat equation (thus, with a non-trivial diffusivity) in a weak sense. This methodology is now commonly referred to as the scaling limit (see Subsection 2.1). Flandoli and Luo [49] subsequently exploited it to establish the global existence of smooth solutions for the 3D NSEs in vorticity form. However, as acknowledged by the same authors in [49, Appendix 3], incorporating transport noise directly into the vorticity formulation lacks a clear physical motivation. The scaling limit technique has subsequently been widely employed across a broad range of SPDEs, yielding several results on regularization by noise [1, 6, 45, 79], mixing and enhanced dissipation [47, 88, 89], and anomalous dissipation [3, 27, 64]. The mechanism exploited in [49] is reminiscent of classical stabilization by noise results [16, 17], as well as the suppression of blow-up by deterministic vector fields with suitable mixing properties [72, 76]. The latter line of research finds its roots in the seminal work of Constantin, Kiselev, Ryzhik, and Zlatoˇ s [32]. The reader is referred to [33] and the references therein for subsequent related results in the deterministic setting. Further progress in the context of scaling limits was achieved by the author in [2], where, by combining maximal Lp-regularity methods for SPDEs pioneered by Krylov [77] and van Neerven, Veraar, and Weis [94], the analogue of Theorem 1.1 was established when the Laplace operator in the stochastic NSEs (1.1) is replaced by a hyperviscous operator ´p´ ∆qα with α ą 1 (see also [5]). The former provides a significant improvement in the well-posedness for the 3D hyperviscous NSEs compared to the deterministic theory, where global regularity results are limited to the case 

α ě 54 (so-called J.L. Lions exponent [82]), or more recently, α « 54 , see [30, 31]. However, the presence of hyperviscosity breaks the natural scaling of the NSEs (1.1) (see Subsection 2.1), and transport noise becomes a lower-order perturbation along the scaling limit (see [2, Section 3] for details). For the true stochastic NSEs (1.1), the approach in [2] breaks down. Indeed, the Laplace operator and the transport noise are of the same differential order (see [9, Subsection 1.1] for the explicit scaling argument), and therefore, the latter cannot be treated via perturbation techniques. Overcoming this difficulty requires the develop-ment of entirely new machinery, such as stochastic Caccioppoli inequalities, localized stochastic maximal regularity, quantitative localized scaling limits, and new supre-mum bounds for SPDEs with highly oscillating coefficients. Moreover, our investiga-tion reveals novel structural connections between scaling limits and homogenization theory (we refer to the recent work of Armstrong and Vicol [15] for another applica-tion of homogenization techniques to anomalous dissipation in fluid mechanics). A thorough discussion of the novelty and strategy is given in the following section. ABSENCE OF BLOW-UP IN 3D NSEs WITH TRANSPORT NOISE 7

Figure 1. Proof architecture. The main result (Theorem 4.1) and the crucial intermediate step (Theorem 9.4) are in bold. Relevant subsections are indicated in parentheses. 2. Proof outline 

This section provides an overview of the proof strategy for Theorem 4.1, which generalizes Theorem 1.1. The architecture is summarized in Figure 1 and proceeds via several steps. We present the strategy in reverse order, starting from the main theorem and tracing back to the fundamental estimates, to underline the role of each ingredient. First, we explain how the regularization by noise for the 3D NSEs can be established via certain LppLq q-estimates. Second, we introduce the central mathematical novelty of this paper: supremum moment bounds for the stochastic oscillating Stokes system (Subsection 2.2). These bounds are derived by combining 8 ANTONIO AGRESTI 

localized stochastic maximal Lp-regularity with uniform control of the energy at mi-croscopic scales . Finally, we present the main tools to obtain such uniform bounds at microscopic scales, namely, stochastic Caccioppoli inequalities, local smoothing for the homogenized Stokes system, and quantitative local scaling limits. To establish the supremum moments estimates fundamental to our approach, we cast the classical large-scale regularity and blow-up ideas of Avellaneda and Lin [18] into a novel quantitative stochastic framework (Subsection 2.3). Implementing this approach requires overcoming two fundamental obstructions: 

‚ Breakdown of qualitative methods . Compactness techniques fail in the sto-chastic setting, as enforcing strong convergence in the probability space en-tails a loss of integrability, which destroys the propagation of bounds across spatial scales. Consequently, we establish quantitative error estimates for the oscillating Stokes system relative to its homogenized counterpart (see the end of Subsection 2.3). 

‚ Non-locality and time irregularity of the pressures . The deterministic and stochastic pressures provide several additional complications, particularly due to their non-local nature and lack of time regularization. To handle them, we follow in spirit the celebrated work of Caffarelli, Kohn, and Nirenberg [28], utilizing local solutions, local energy inequalities, and local approximations of the pressures to capture their smoothness (Subsection 2.5). We begin by discussing the relevant background. For any unexplained notation, see Subsection 2.6 below. 2.1. Background: Criticality, scaling limits and LppLq q-estimates. The deterministic and stochastic pressures contribute non-trivially to (2.11), from both dynamical and mathematical points of view. For clarity of exposition, we post-pone comments on their analysis to Subsection 2.5.

LppLq q-estimates with parameters as in the second condition of (1.3). 2.3. Large-scale regularity, blow-up and cumulative scale. This subsection outlines the core ideas behind the uniform-in-N energy bounds at the microscopic scale (2.18). We revisit, in this context, the arguments of Avellaneda and Lin [18] used to prove optimal regularity in the homogenization of elliptic PDEs, which are rooted in the study of minimal surfaces [57]. The reader is referred to [101, Subsection 4.1] for a streamlined exposition. A discussion on the breakdown of qualitative methods 14 ANTONIO AGRESTI 

and difficulties related to the pressures in (2.11) is postponed to Subsections 2.4 and 2.5, respectively. 

Homogenization and large-scale regularity. The core intuition driving this energy con-trol at the cutoff scale N ´1Q is as follows. As N Ñ 8 , the averaged norm L2pN ´1Qq

shrinks to a pointwise evaluation. Simultaneously, following the discussion in Sub-section 2.1, the solution vN is homogenizing towards the solution to 

Btv “ ´ ∇π ` `1 ` 35 μ˘∆v ` ∇ ¨ `F ´ 25 μ G ˘ on Q. (2.21) From parabolic regularity, and under suitable assumptions on π (see Subsection 2.5), 

v is smooth on a (say) half parabolic cylinder p1{2qQ. Consequently, pointwise eval-uation at the center pt0, x 0q is well defined. Therefore, one might hope for a delicate compensation between the emerging smoothness of vN and the singular nature of the norm L2pN ´1Qq. This is obtained below by following the ideas of [18]. From this perspective, achieving uniform control of the energy (2.18) down to the microscopic scale naturally translates into a large-scale regularity result for the highly oscillatory system (2.11), in the sense that one can “access” the energy of the solution

In the following section, we describe the quantitative error estimates in the homoge-nization of vN,δ towards vN,δ and the emergence of the cumulative scale L “ δN .2.4. Caccioppoli inequalities and quantitative localized scaling limits. Clos-ing the one-step improvement (2.23) relies on two fundamental analytical ingredients: Stochastic Caccioppoli inequalities and quantitative local scaling limits. 

Stochastic Caccioppoli inequalities. Broadly speaking, stochastic Caccioppoli esti-mates are “reverse Poincar´ e” inequalities with nested spatial support, and they hold for elliptic and parabolic problems without any regularity assumptions beyond bound-edness and measurability. For these reasons, they are an indispensable tool in homog-enization theory [101]. For the problem (2.28), the stochastic Caccioppoli inequality takes the form (see Section 5):

The key point in the above estimate is that the constant C does not depend on the oscillation parameters N and δ. This is essential to bound the error (2.31). As shown in (2.34)-(2.35) below, the error explicitly depends on the macroscopic gradient ∇vN,δ 

on p1{2qQ. At this large scale, the system remains highly oscillatory, precluding any uniform smoothness (see Figure 2). Conversely, the one-step improvement (2.23) requires an estimate formulated only in terms of local oscillations. The Caccioppoli inequality (2.32) thus serves as a fundamental bridge: it bounds the gradients required by the error estimates entirely in terms of the L2-oscillations demanded by the one-step improvement. The proof of (2.32) relies on local energy estimates in the spirit of Scheffer [99] and Caffarelli, Kohn, and Nirenberg [28]. In Subsections 2.2-2.4, we discussed the strategy to derive a bound on the veloc-ity field vN solving (2.11). In the following subsection, we analyze the role of the deterministic and stochastic pressures in the previously discussed argument. 2.5. Scaling invariance of the pressures and local approximations. In this subsection, we address the obstructions introduced by the deterministic and stochastic pressures πN,δ and rπN,δ k,α within the previously outlined program (see Figure 1). First, we recall a well-known pathology inherently tied to local solutions of incompressible fluid equations, namely the poor temporal regularity of the pressure, as illustrated by Serrin’s classical counterexample, cf. [80, Subsection 13.1]. This obstruction is naturally present when studying the local regularity of the homogenized Stokes system (2.29). Consequently, we cannot expect any further time regularity in (2.24) for the velocity vN,δ . This deficit directly dictates our strict use of spatial averages —rather than standard space-time averages—in the oscillation decomposition (2.22). However, even more severe complications arise from the contribution of the global pressures to the iterative scheme (2.23)-(2.25) and the deterministic error (2.34). We overcome these difficulties by employing local approximations in the spirit of [28]. Before detailing this, however, we focus on the scaling invariance, which plays a central role in controlling the energy at the microscopic scale (Subsection 2.3). 

Scaling invariance of the pressures. Due to the incompressibility ∇ ¨ vN “ 0 in (2.11), the pressures satisfy the following elliptic problems: ∆πN “ ∇2 : F ´

πNℓ pt, x q “ ηℓπN pη2ℓt, η ℓxq and rπNk,α,ℓ pt, x q “ rπNk,α pη2ℓt, η ℓxq.

}πNℓ }L2pηI ;H´1pηB qq ≂ }πN }L2pη1`ℓI;H´1pη1`ℓBqq (2.39) 

}rπNk,α,ℓ }L2pQq ≂ }rπNk,α }L2pη1`ℓQq,(2.40) where H´1pBq denotes the negative Sobolev space endowed with the averaged norm (see (2.44) below). A similar rescaling also holds for the associated oscillations. The above scale-invariance precisely determines how the pressures enter both the stochastic Caccioppoli inequality and the large-scale regularity estimates (2.23)-(2.25). Consequently, controlling the pressure oscillations measured in the norms of (2.39)-(2.40) becomes an intrinsic part of the iterative scheme (see Section 8). Finally, we remark that in the one-step improvement, due to the lack of temporal smooth-ness, we measure the stochastic pressure in the space Lp0 pI; L2pBqq for some p0 ą 2to ensure the required contraction at the scale η analogous to (2.23). In contrast, for the deterministic pressure, the decay of the H´1pηB q-norm as η Ñ 0 compensates for the lack of time regularity, thus avoiding additional integrability conditions. 20 ANTONIO AGRESTI 

Local approximation and deterministic error in the local scaling limit. Finally, we outline the strategy to bound the deterministic error (2.34) for local solutions to the rescaled system (2.28). Because the global pressures πN,δ and rπN,δ k,α of the rescaled problem (2.28) are inherently non-local, their direct use is precluded. However, the elliptic equations governing these pressures naturally suggest the use of local approx-imations to capture their interior smoothness [28]. For the sake of exposition, we focus here on the stochastic pressure; the deterministic counterpart relies on a simi-lar conceptual localization but is analytically more involved. ‚ (Flexibility in the choice of noise coefficients) The choice of θ in Theorem 4.1 is not unique. Indeed, let θN be as in (3.1) for some fixed a ą 0. Then, it follows from its proof that there exists N0 depending only on p, q, ε, M and 

a such that the pp, q q-solution pu, τ q to the stochastic 3D NSEs (1.1) with 

θ “ θN satisfies (4.3)-(4.5) provided N ě N0.As discussed below Theorem 1.1, the key to understanding this regularizing phe-nomenon lies in the enhanced dissipation effect of transport noise on the Navier-Stokes dynamics. Crucially, solutions to the stochastic 3D NSEs (1.1) satisfy the same path-wise energy balance (1.2) as their deterministic counterparts, regardless of the noise parameters μ and θ. This preservation of energy extends to the spatial deviation from the average: (4.7) 12

.(5.7) As discussed in Subsection 2.4, stochastic Caccioppoli estimates encode a local smoothing effect due to parabolicity and can be thought of as “reverse Poincar´ e” estimates. The choice of the spaces for the pressure is consistent with the scaling of the stochastic Stokes system (5.1), see Subsection 2.5. Clearly, by splitting the complex noise into its real and imaginary parts as in (3.9), the real-valued abstract system (5.1) exactly recovers the oscillating Stokes systems discussed in Section 2. Thus, the above stochastic Caccioppoli inequality applies with constants uniform in the parameters determining the oscillations for the stochastic Stokes systems in (2.11) and (2.28). Let us point out that, differently from the Caccioppoli inequal-ity of Theorem 5.2, the above result relies on the smoothness assumption on the noise coefficients pζnqn in (6.3). Theorem 6.1 is proved in Subsection 6.2.3 and is a localized version of [9, Theorem 3.2]. While the first condition (6.2) is necessary for (6.4) to hold, we expect that (6.3) can be weakened to pζnqn P Cδ pQ; ℓ2q for some δ ą 0 as for the global-in-space stochastic maximal Lp-regularity estimates in [9, Section 3]. In the proof of Theorem 6.1, we also obtain local regularity estimates for the pressures π and rπn.While local smoothing for the pressures is central in obtaining the bound (6.4), these estimates will not be used in the following sections, and therefore we do not state them explicitly. The interested reader is referred to Lemmas 6.5 and 6.6 below. 6.2. Proof of Theorem 6.1. The proof of the localized stochastic maximal Lp-estimate (6.4) for local solutions of the stochastic Stokes system (6.1) relies on an iterative argument, in which at each step we gain a small amount of regularity by shrinking the domain. The proof of the iterative regularity gain is divided into three steps: 

‚ Local gain of smoothness for the velocity field – Lemma 6.4. 

‚ Local gain of smoothness for the stochastic pressure – Lemma 6.5. 

‚ Local gain of smoothness for the deterministic pressure – Lemma 6.6. This division is dictated by the intrinsic structure of the stochastic Stokes system (6.1). We emphasize that this strategy differs from the one used to prove Caccioppoli inequalities in Section 5, as it relies crucially on the additional regularity assumption (6.3) enforced in this section. 6.2.1. Local smoothing for the velocity field. In this subsection, we discuss local smooth-ing effects for the velocity field v in (6.1). The following can be seen as a local version of [9, Theorem 3.2].

7.2. Extrapolated spaces and Helmholtz projection on domains. In this sub-section, we provide a brief introduction to extrapolation spaces for sectorial operators. A complete treatment can be found in either [13, Chapter V.1] or [61, Subsection 6.3]. In a nutshell, extrapolation spaces for an operator A (e.g., the Laplacian with Dirich-let boundary conditions on L2pBq) can be thought of as the analogue of negative Sobolev spaces on domains, with the additional requirement that A still preserves the spectral properties on such extrapolated spaces (e.g., still generates an analytic semigroup, possesses maximal Lp-regularity, etc.). It is well-known that, in the case where A is the Dirichlet Laplacian on H´2pBq, A does not generate a semigroup and, in a very weak sense, boundary conditions have to be taken into account. More-over, as commented in Subsection 2.4, they provide the right framework to show local mixing estimates for the noise in (7.1). We divide this subsection into two parts. In the first, we provide a brief intro-duction to the extrapolated scale in the context of self-adjoint operators on Hilbert spaces (for this case, see also [103, Chapter 5, Appendix A]), together with some basic properties. In the second, we discuss the important relation between the ex-trapolated spaces associated with the Dirichlet Laplacian and the Stokes operator, as well as consequences for the Helmholtz projection. 7.2.1. Extrapolated spaces. Let A : DpAq Ď X Ñ X be a positive, invertible, and self-adjoint operator on a Hilbert space X with domain DpAq. For any σ ă 0, we define the extrapolated scale X Aσ as (7.26) pX Aσ , } ¨ } X Aσ q def 

“ p X, }Aσ ¨ } X q„

where „ denotes the completion of the normed space and Aσ denotes the fractional power of the operator A, see e.g., [68, Subsection 15.2]. For the case of self-adjoint operators, to define fractional powers, one can equivalently use the spectral theorem [104, Chapter 8, Section 2]. From the invertibility assumption of A, it follows that 

A´σ is injective, and therefore X Aσ is well-defined. One can also complete the scale for non-negative smoothness by letting (7.27) X A

À } φ}C2pBq}p ∇hk,α qk,α }L2p2B;ℓ2q,

where the last inequality follows from (7.71) and Fubini’s theorem. Arguing as in the proof of Proposition 7.11(1), by Weyl’s law, the above proves the estimate in Proposition 7.11(3) for σ0 sufficiently large, depending only on d. As mentioned above, the conclusion follows from complex interpolation and the uniform bound (7.74). □

Comparing (8.8) with the regularity in Proposition 7.1, it follows that the velocity field vN,δ gains regularity far from the boundary, which is the roughness source. This is consistent with the well-known fact that the regularity of solutions to elliptic and parabolic PDEs depends only locally on the coefficients and data. However, due to the limited time regularity of πN,δ (see Proposition 7.1), the above local smoothing is non-standard, and in particular, we do not expect a control BtvN,δ 

in Lppp I{4q; H´1,q pp 1{4qBqq that is the maximal time regularity counterpart of (8.8). This lack of temporal regularity in the homogenized velocity explains why, in the one-step improvement of Lemma 8.3, we study velocity oscillations within spatial balls rather than the time-space cylinders typically used in parabolic PDEs.

The key obstacle is the lack of smoothness of H, which was of key importance in Theorem 7.2, see Subsection 7.4. We first outline the main idea behind the proof of Lemma 7.13. Our method differs significantly from the Fourier-based techniques of Flandoli and Luo [49, Appendix A] (cf. [88, Appendix A]), which are limited to periodic boundary conditions. Here, we work on the spatial domain rather than frequency space. The primary advantage of our approach is that it makes the transition from the non-local Itˆ o-Stratonovich correctors to their local limit completely transparent.
